========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in is... well, it mostly happens late at night. Once I start picking at the leftovers in the fridge, it feels like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, 'That’s enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's usually whatever is in the Tupperware. You know, they really don't make Tupperware like they used to. We bought a set back in the eighties—the orange and brown ones, you remember those?—and those things lasted forever. Now you buy the plastic containers at the supermarket and they crack after two runs in the dishwasher. We actually had to replace our dishwasher just last month. The fellow who came to install it was practically a kid, maybe twenty years old, and he didn't even use a level when he pushed it under the counter. I had to get my own tools out after he left. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the leftovers. Yes, I just can't seem to stop my hand from reaching back in once I get started.

Yes, I guess I do. Some nights after supper, when I’m supposed to be putting away the tamales or the mole, I’ll just keep at it. I'll end up eating several servings, and then I'll take a bunch of pan dulce out to the garage... it's definitely way more than I’d ever put on a normal plate if my wife or the family was watching. 

I go out to the garage because I've got my old workbench out there. I've been trying to organize my tools for months. You know, I bought this beautiful Craftsman tool chest back in '95, heavy steel, none of that cheap aluminum they use now. The drawers glide so smooth. I was actually looking for my socket wrench the other day because the lawnmower started making this awful rattling noise. It sounded like a bag of rocks. Turns out a loose bolt was just vibrating against the casing. Took me twenty minutes to fix it, but by then it was too hot to mow anyway. The summers here are getting ridiculous, don't you think? I used to be able to do yard work at noon, but now if you're not out there by 7 AM, you're roasted. 

But... oh, I'm sorry, you asked about the eating. Yes, I'll eat all those tamales and the sweet bread out there in just an hour or two, and I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.

I don't know if I'd use the word ashamed. I haven't really noticed feeling guilty about it, to be honest. Mostly I just feel really tired afterwards. 

Speaking of being tired, my sleep has been completely out of whack lately. No, I don't do anything like that. I don't make myself throw up or take anything, and I certainly don't exercise. 

My exercising days are pretty much behind me, especially with this knee of mine. I used to jog, you know, back in the late seventies. I had this pair of blue and yellow Adidas running shoes. They don't make shoes like that anymore. Everything now is neon colors and looks like a spaceship. I remember I used to run around the reservoir every morning before breakfast. There was this one older fellow who was always out there walking his dog, a basset hound, I think it was. That dog had the longest ears I've ever seen, practically dragging on the pavement. I always wondered how the poor thing didn't trip over itself. We'd always wave to each other. The man, not the dog, obviously. 

But no... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, making myself sick or exercising. No, I don't do any of that after I eat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, a lot of the time I'm not even hungry. I mean, I've already had a good dinner with everyone, you know? My wife makes these incredible enchiladas on Tuesdays, the recipe is from her grandmother down in Sonora. But later on, I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another. 

Speaking of containers, I was just at the hardware store looking for some good plastic bins for the garage. Everything is so flimsy nowadays. You buy a bin, put a few heavy wrenches in it, and the bottom cracks the second you pick it up. I remember when rubbermaid actually made things out of thick rubber. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You really had to put your shoulder into it to buff it out, but the shine was unbelievable. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps from across the street. 

Anyway... where was I? Oh, the food. Right. Yeah, I'm usually not hungry at all when I start eating all that extra stuff in the evening, since I just had dinner.

Well, I suppose so, yeah. It happens a lot when I'm supposed to be cleaning up after supper. Like, I'll be out in the kitchen putting away the leftover tamales or the mole, and before I know it, I've eaten several servings right there standing at the counter. And then I'll take a whole box of pan dulce out to the garage with me. It's definitely more than I'd ever put on a plate if my wife or the kids were sitting there watching me. 

Speaking of the kids, my youngest, Mateo, he just bought his first house out in Henderson. It's a nice place, stucco, tile roof, but the landscaping is a complete disaster. The previous owners just let the Bermuda grass take over everything. Bermuda grass is impossible to get rid of once it gets established. You can dig it up, spray it, do whatever you want, but if you leave even one little piece of a runner in the ground, it comes right back. I told him he needs to rent one of those sod cutters, but he insists he can just smother it with cardboard and mulch. I tried that back in '92 when we lived on Elm Street. We had this huge oak tree in the front yard that dropped leaves everywhere, and I thought the cardboard trick would save me some raking. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. 

But anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. Once I start on those tamales, I just keep going, and I can't really seem to stop myself until it's all gone.

My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, looking back on it, I'd say these late-night leftover episodes have been happening at least once most weeks, often two nights a week. It really started up since my cousin moved in with us. 

He's a good guy, my cousin Arturo, but he brought all these boxes with him. Just towers of cardboard in the spare room. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. I remember when I was a kid, every grocery store had a tube tester machine right by the front door, next to the gumball machines. You'd pull the bad tube out of your TV, take it down to the Piggly Wiggly, plug it into the sockets on the machine to see if the needle went to the red or the green, and then buy a replacement right out of the cabinet underneath. It was so simple. Now, if a TV breaks, you just throw the whole flat screen in the dumpster. It's a real shame, all that waste. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, how often. Yeah, since Arturo moved in, it's been about once or twice a week that I end up eating all that food and can't stop.

I told him, \"Listen, I don't want a bed that remembers me, I want a bed with good old-fashioned springs.\" We ended up getting a firm one, but getting it up the stairs was an absolute nightmare. My lower back hasn't been the same since I helped my brother-in-law build his deck back in 2018. We used that composite decking, you know, the stuff that's supposed to never rot? Well, the manufacturer doesn't tell you how incredibly heavy it is. We were out there in the blazing sun lugging those boards around for three days straight. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling upset. No, I don't really feel upset about the eating. I just sort of clean up the garage and head to bed.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or try to exercise a lot extra to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I don't make myself throw up or take anything, and I certainly don't exercise. \n\nMy exercising days are pretty much behind me, especially with this knee of mine. I used to jog, you know, back in the late seventies. I had this pair of blue and yellow Adidas running shoes. They don't make shoes like that anymore. Everything now is neon colors and looks like a spaceship. I remember I used to run around the reservoir every morning before breakfast. There was this one older fellow who was always out there walking his dog, a basset hound, I think it was. That dog had the longest ears I've ever seen, practically dragging on the pavement. I always wondered how the poor thing didn't trip over itself. We'd always wave to each other. The man, not the dog, obviously. \n\nBut no... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, making myself sick or exercising. No, I don't do any of that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating large amounts of food even when you’re not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, a lot of the time I'm not even hungry. I mean, I've already had a good dinner with everyone, you know? My wife makes these incredible enchiladas on Tuesdays, the recipe is from her grandmother down in Sonora. But later on, I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another. \n\nSpeaking of containers, I was just at the hardware store looking for some good plastic bins for the garage. Everything is so flimsy nowadays. You buy a bin, put a few heavy wrenches in it, and the bottom cracks the second you pick it up. I remember when rubbermaid actually made things out of thick rubber. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You really had to put your shoulder into it to buff it out, but the shine was unbelievable. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps from across the street. \n\nAnyway... where was I? Oh, the food. Right. Yeah, I'm usually not hungry at all when I start eating all that extra stuff in the evening, since I just had dinner.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, during these episodes, you feel like you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and that you can't stop yourself once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yeah. It happens a lot when I'm supposed to be cleaning up after supper. Like, I'll be out in the kitchen putting away the leftover tamales or the mole, and before I know it, I've eaten several servings right there standing at the counter. And then I'll take a whole box of pan dulce out to the garage with me. It's definitely more than I'd ever put on a plate if my wife or the kids were sitting there watching me. \n\nSpeaking of the kids, my youngest, Mateo, he just bought his first house out in Henderson. It's a nice place, stucco, tile roof, but the landscaping is a complete disaster. The previous owners just let the Bermuda grass take over everything. Bermuda grass is impossible to get rid of once it gets established. You can dig it up, spray it, do whatever you want, but if you leave even one little piece of a runner in the ground, it comes right back. I told him he needs to rent one of those sod cutters, but he insists he can just smother it with cardboard and mulch. I tried that back in '92 when we lived on Elm Street. We had this huge oak tree in the front yard that dropped leaves everywhere, and I thought the cardboard trick would save me some raking. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. Once I start on those tamales, I just keep going, and I can't really seem to stop myself until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and feeling like you can’t stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back on it, I'd say these late-night leftover episodes have been happening at least once most weeks, often two nights a week. It really started up since my cousin moved in with us. \n\nHe's a good guy, my cousin Arturo, but he brought all these boxes with him. Just towers of cardboard in the spare room. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. I remember when I was a kid, every grocery store had a tube tester machine right by the front door, next to the gumball machines. You'd pull the bad tube out of your TV, take it down to the Piggly Wiggly, plug it into the sockets on the machine to see if the needle went to the red or the green, and then buy a replacement right out of the cabinet underneath. It was so simple. Now, if a TV breaks, you just throw the whole flat screen in the dumpster. It's a real shame, all that waste. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, how often. Yeah, since Arturo moved in, it's been about once or twice a week that I end up eating all that food and can't stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. We had this huge oak tree in the front yard that dropped leaves everywhere, and I thought the cardboard trick would save me some raking. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. Once I start on those tamales, I just keep going, and I can't really seem to stop myself until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and feeling like you can’t stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back on it, I'd say these late-night leftover episodes have been happening at least once most weeks, often two nights a week. It really started up since my cousin moved in with us. \n\nHe's a good guy, my cousin Arturo, but he brought all these boxes with him. Just towers of cardboard in the spare room. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. I remember when I was a kid, every grocery store had a tube tester machine right by the front door, next to the gumball machines. You'd pull the bad tube out of your TV, take it down to the Piggly Wiggly, plug it into the sockets on the machine to see if the needle went to the red or the green, and then buy a replacement right out of the cabinet underneath. It was so simple. Now, if a TV breaks, you just throw the whole flat screen in the dumpster. It's a real shame, all that waste. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, how often. Yeah, since Arturo moved in, it's been about once or twice a week that I end up eating all that food and can't stop.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these episodes of eating a lot in a short time cause you significant distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still keeping up with my errands, appointments, and helping around the house, but the mornings after it happens I feel sluggish and less proud of how I’m handling myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it definitely bothers me. I mean, I'm still keeping up with everything I need to do—running my errands, going to my appointments, doing my chores around the house. But the mornings after I do this, I just feel so sluggish, you know? And I feel... well, not very proud of how I'm handling myself. 

Speaking of errands, I had to go down to the DMV yesterday to renew my registration. What a nightmare that place is. You stand in one line just to get a number, so you can sit in a plastic chair for two hours waiting for them to call that number, just to tell you that you need a different form. And the forms! They ask for everything short of your blood type. I brought my utility bill, my property tax statement, my old registration, and the lady still looked at me like I was trying to pull a fast one. It reminds me of when I tried to get a passport back in the 80s for a trip to Cabo. We had this travel agent, Brenda, who swore she had it all figured out, but she booked us on this tiny puddle-jumper plane. The turbulence was so bad my wife spilled her ginger ale all over the guy next to us. He was wearing this expensive-looking silk shirt, too. Silk is the worst for stains, you basically have to take it to the dry cleaner immediately or it's ruined forever. 

Anyway... yeah, I still get my stuff done, but I feel pretty terrible and sluggish the next day after I eat like that. It really gets me down.

My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You stand in one line just to get a number, so you can sit in a plastic chair for two hours waiting for them to call that number, just to tell you that you need a different form. And the forms! They ask for everything short of your blood type. I brought my utility bill, my property tax statement, my old registration, and the lady still looked at me like I was trying to pull a fast one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I definitely do that. I make sure I wait until the house is completely quiet, or I'll take the food out to the garage. The garage is kind of my sanctuary anyway, you know? I've got my workbench out there, all my tools perfectly organized on this pegboard I put up back in... gosh, must have been 2005. 

I used to buy all my tools at Sears. It's a real shame what happened to Sears. You used to be able to walk in there, buy a Craftsman wrench, and if it ever broke, they'd replace it for free, no questions asked. I remember my dad had this huge stack of Sears catalogs in the basement. We'd look through the Christmas Wish Book every November, circling the toys we wanted. You just don't get that same feeling clicking around on the internet nowadays. The mail carrier used to complain about how heavy those catalogs were, though. Our mailman back then, Gary, he had this bad knee from playing college football, so he hated carrying anything heavier than a postcard. He wore these thick braces on both legs, sounded like the Tin Man walking up our driveway. 

Anyway... sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Oh, right, eating in the garage. Yeah, I go out there or wait until everyone is asleep because I'd just be so embarrassed for my cousin or my family to see how much I'm eating. I'm the oldest brother, you know? I'm supposed to be the disciplined one. So I just hide it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in is... well, it mostly happens late at night. Once I start picking at the leftovers in the fridge, it feels like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, 'That’s enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's usually whatever is in the Tupperware. You know, they really don't make Tupperware like they used to. We bought a set back in the eighties—the orange and brown ones, you remember those?—and those things lasted forever. Now you buy the plastic containers at the supermarket and they crack after two runs in the dishwasher. We actually had to replace our dishwasher just last month. The fellow who came to install it was practically a kid, maybe twenty years old, and he didn't even use a level when he pushed it under the counter. I had to get my own tools out after he left. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the leftovers. Yes, I just can't seem to stop my hand from reaching back in once I get started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself eating a lot more food than most people would in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours, and feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do. Some nights after supper, when I’m supposed to be putting away the tamales or the mole, I’ll just keep at it. I'll end up eating several servings, and then I'll take a bunch of pan dulce out to the garage... it's definitely way more than I’d ever put on a normal plate if my wife or the family was watching. \n\nI go out to the garage because I've got my old workbench out there. I've been trying to organize my tools for months. You know, I bought this beautiful Craftsman tool chest back in '95, heavy steel, none of that cheap aluminum they use now. The drawers glide so smooth. I was actually looking for my socket wrench the other day because the lawnmower started making this awful rattling noise. It sounded like a bag of rocks. Turns out a loose bolt was just vibrating against the casing. Took me twenty minutes to fix it, but by then it was too hot to mow anyway. The summers here are getting ridiculous, don't you think? I used to be able to do yard work at noon, but now if you're not out there by 7 AM, you're roasted. \n\nBut... oh, I'm sorry, you asked about the eating. Yes, I'll eat all those tamales and the sweet bread out there in just an hour or two, and I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset, ashamed, or guilty after these episodes of eating more than you intended to?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd use the word ashamed. I haven't really noticed feeling guilty about it, to be honest. Mostly I just feel really tired afterwards. \n\nSpeaking of being tired, my sleep has been completely out of whack lately. I thought it was our old mattress causing it, so my wife and I went down to that big mattress liquidator on Highway 9 last Tuesday. The salesman there—real pushy fellow, wearing a suit that was at least a size too small—kept trying to sell us on this expensive memory foam stuff. I told him, \"Listen, I don't want a bed that remembers me, I want a bed with good old-fashioned springs.\" We ended up getting a firm one, but getting it up the stairs was an absolute nightmare. My lower back hasn't been the same since I helped my brother-in-law build his deck back in 2018. We used that composite decking, you know, the stuff that's supposed to never rot? Well, the manufacturer doesn't tell you how incredibly heavy it is. We were out there in the blazing sun lugging those boards around for three days straight. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling upset. No, I don't really feel upset about the eating. I just sort of clean up the garage and head to bed.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or try to exercise a lot extra to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I don't make myself throw up or take anything, and I certainly don't exercise. \n\nMy exercising days are pretty much behind me, especially with this knee of mine. I used to jog, you know, back in the late seventies. I had this pair of blue and yellow Adidas running shoes. They don't make shoes like that anymore. Everything now is neon colors and looks like a spaceship. I remember I used to run around the reservoir every morning before breakfast. There was this one older fellow who was always out there walking his dog, a basset hound, I think it was. That dog had the longest ears I've ever seen, practically dragging on the pavement. I always wondered how the poor thing didn't trip over itself. We'd always wave to each other. The man, not the dog, obviously. \n\nBut no... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, making myself sick or exercising. No, I don't do any of that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating large amounts of food even when you’re not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, a lot of the time I'm not even hungry. I mean, I've already had a good dinner with everyone, you know? My wife makes these incredible enchiladas on Tuesdays, the recipe is from her grandmother down in Sonora. But later on, I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another. \n\nSpeaking of containers, I was just at the hardware store looking for some good plastic bins for the garage. Everything is so flimsy nowadays. You buy a bin, put a few heavy wrenches in it, and the bottom cracks the second you pick it up. I remember when rubbermaid actually made things out of thick rubber. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You really had to put your shoulder into it to buff it out, but the shine was unbelievable. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps from across the street. \n\nAnyway... where was I? Oh, the food. Right. Yeah, I'm usually not hungry at all when I start eating all that extra stuff in the evening, since I just had dinner.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, during these episodes, you feel like you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and that you can't stop yourself once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yeah. It happens a lot when I'm supposed to be cleaning up after supper. Like, I'll be out in the kitchen putting away the leftover tamales or the mole, and before I know it, I've eaten several servings right there standing at the counter. And then I'll take a whole box of pan dulce out to the garage with me. It's definitely more than I'd ever put on a plate if my wife or the kids were sitting there watching me. \n\nSpeaking of the kids, my youngest, Mateo, he just bought his first house out in Henderson. It's a nice place, stucco, tile roof, but the landscaping is a complete disaster. The previous owners just let the Bermuda grass take over everything. Bermuda grass is impossible to get rid of once it gets established. You can dig it up, spray it, do whatever you want, but if you leave even one little piece of a runner in the ground, it comes right back. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. Have they occurred regularly, at least once a week for several months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 4 months, since my older cousin came to live with us.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let's see, it's been going on for about four months now. It started right around the time my older cousin Arturo came to live with us. 

Arturo, he had to move out of his old place because the landlord decided to sell the building. It was one of those beautiful old brick duplexes over on Elm Street. You know the ones? They have those big wrap-around porches and the stained glass windows above the front door. They just don't build houses like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap drywall and plastic siding that blows off in a stiff wind. My grandfather was a bricklayer, actually. He came over from Italy in the twenties and helped build half the municipal buildings downtown. He used to say that a good brick wall should last a hundred years, easy. He had hands like sandpaper, though. When he'd pat you on the cheek, it felt like a cat's tongue. He used to make his own wine in the basement, too. Smelled awful, like vinegar and old socks, but he drank a little glass of it every single night with his dinner. Claimed it was good for the heart. I don't know about that, but he lived to be ninety-two, so maybe he was onto something. 

But, uh... yeah, four months. It's been pretty regular since he moved in, at least once a week, just like I was saying before.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in is... well, it mostly happens late at night. Once I start picking at the leftovers in the fridge, it feels like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, 'That’s enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's usually whatever is in the Tupperware. You know, they really don't make Tupperware like they used to. We bought a set back in the eighties—the orange and brown ones, you remember those?—and those things lasted forever. Now you buy the plastic containers at the supermarket and they crack after two runs in the dishwasher. We actually had to replace our dishwasher just last month. The fellow who came to install it was practically a kid, maybe twenty years old, and he didn't even use a level when he pushed it under the counter. I had to get my own tools out after he left. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the leftovers. Yes, I just can't seem to stop my hand from reaching back in once I get started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself eating a lot more food than most people would in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours, and feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do. Some nights after supper, when I’m supposed to be putting away the tamales or the mole, I’ll just keep at it. I'll end up eating several servings, and then I'll take a bunch of pan dulce out to the garage... it's definitely way more than I’d ever put on a normal plate if my wife or the family was watching. \n\nI go out to the garage because I've got my old workbench out there. I've been trying to organize my tools for months. You know, I bought this beautiful Craftsman tool chest back in '95, heavy steel, none of that cheap aluminum they use now. The drawers glide so smooth. I was actually looking for my socket wrench the other day because the lawnmower started making this awful rattling noise. It sounded like a bag of rocks. Turns out a loose bolt was just vibrating against the casing. Took me twenty minutes to fix it, but by then it was too hot to mow anyway. The summers here are getting ridiculous, don't you think? I used to be able to do yard work at noon, but now if you're not out there by 7 AM, you're roasted. \n\nBut... oh, I'm sorry, you asked about the eating. Yes, I'll eat all those tamales and the sweet bread out there in just an hour or two, and I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset, ashamed, or guilty after these episodes of eating more than you intended to?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd use the word ashamed. I haven't really noticed feeling guilty about it, to be honest. Mostly I just feel really tired afterwards. \n\nSpeaking of being tired, my sleep has been completely out of whack lately. I thought it was our old mattress causing it, so my wife and I went down to that big mattress liquidator on Highway 9 last Tuesday. The salesman there—real pushy fellow, wearing a suit that was at least a size too small—kept trying to sell us on this expensive memory foam stuff. I told him, \"Listen, I don't want a bed that remembers me, I want a bed with good old-fashioned springs.\" We ended up getting a firm one, but getting it up the stairs was an absolute nightmare. My lower back hasn't been the same since I helped my brother-in-law build his deck back in 2018. We used that composite decking, you know, the stuff that's supposed to never rot? Well, the manufacturer doesn't tell you how incredibly heavy it is. We were out there in the blazing sun lugging those boards around for three days straight. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling upset. No, I don't really feel upset about the eating. I just sort of clean up the garage and head to bed.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or try to exercise a lot extra to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I don't make myself throw up or take anything, and I certainly don't exercise. \n\nMy exercising days are pretty much behind me, especially with this knee of mine. I used to jog, you know, back in the late seventies. I had this pair of blue and yellow Adidas running shoes. They don't make shoes like that anymore. Everything now is neon colors and looks like a spaceship. I remember I used to run around the reservoir every morning before breakfast. There was this one older fellow who was always out there walking his dog, a basset hound, I think it was. That dog had the longest ears I've ever seen, practically dragging on the pavement. I always wondered how the poor thing didn't trip over itself. We'd always wave to each other. The man, not the dog, obviously. \n\nBut no... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, making myself sick or exercising. No, I don't do any of that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating large amounts of food even when you’re not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, a lot of the time I'm not even hungry. I mean, I've already had a good dinner with everyone, you know? My wife makes these incredible enchiladas on Tuesdays, the recipe is from her grandmother down in Sonora. But later on, I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another. \n\nSpeaking of containers, I was just at the hardware store looking for some good plastic bins for the garage. Everything is so flimsy nowadays. You buy a bin, put a few heavy wrenches in it, and the bottom cracks the second you pick it up. I remember when rubbermaid actually made things out of thick rubber. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You really had to put your shoulder into it to buff it out, but the shine was unbelievable. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps from across the street. \n\nAnyway... where was I? Oh, the food. Right. Yeah, I'm usually not hungry at all when I start eating all that extra stuff in the evening, since I just had dinner.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, during these episodes, you feel like you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and that you can't stop yourself once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yeah. It happens a lot when I'm supposed to be cleaning up after supper. Like, I'll be out in the kitchen putting away the leftover tamales or the mole, and before I know it, I've eaten several servings right there standing at the counter. And then I'll take a whole box of pan dulce out to the garage with me. It's definitely more than I'd ever put on a plate if my wife or the kids were sitting there watching me. \n\nSpeaking of the kids, my youngest, Mateo, he just bought his first house out in Henderson. It's a nice place, stucco, tile roof, but the landscaping is a complete disaster. The previous owners just let the Bermuda grass take over everything. Bermuda grass is impossible to get rid of once it gets established. You can dig it up, spray it, do whatever you want, but if you leave even one little piece of a runner in the ground, it comes right back. I told him he needs to rent one of those sod cutters, but he insists he can just smother it with cardboard and mulch. I tried that back in '92 when we lived on Elm Street. We had this huge oak tree in the front yard that dropped leaves everywhere, and I thought the cardboard trick would save me some raking. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. Once I start on those tamales, I just keep going, and I can't really seem to stop myself until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and feeling like you can’t stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back on it, I'd say these late-night leftover episodes have been happening at least once most weeks, often two nights a week. It really started up since my cousin moved in with us. \n\nHe's a good guy, my cousin Arturo, but he brought all these boxes with him. Just towers of cardboard in the spare room. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. I remember when I was a kid, every grocery store had a tube tester machine right by the front door, next to the gumball machines. You'd pull the bad tube out of your TV, take it down to the Piggly Wiggly, plug it into the sockets on the machine to see if the needle went to the red or the green, and then buy a replacement right out of the cabinet underneath. It was so simple. Now, if a TV breaks, you just throw the whole flat screen in the dumpster. It's a real shame, all that waste. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, how often. Yeah, since Arturo moved in, it's been about once or twice a week that I end up eating all that food and can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot in a short time cause you significant distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bothers me. I mean, I'm still keeping up with everything I need to do—running my errands, going to my appointments, doing my chores around the house. But the mornings after I do this, I just feel so sluggish, you know? And I feel... well, not very proud of how I'm handling myself. \n\nSpeaking of errands, I had to go down to the DMV yesterday to renew my registration. What a nightmare that place is. You stand in one line just to get a number, so you can sit in a plastic chair for two hours waiting for them to call that number, just to tell you that you need a different form. And the forms! They ask for everything short of your blood type. I brought my utility bill, my property tax statement, my old registration, and the lady still looked at me like I was trying to pull a fast one. It reminds me of when I tried to get a passport back in the 80s for a trip to Cabo. We had this travel agent, Brenda, who swore she had it all figured out, but she booked us on this tiny puddle-jumper plane. The turbulence was so bad my wife spilled her ginger ale all over the guy next to us. He was wearing this expensive-looking silk shirt, too. Silk is the worst for stains, you basically have to take it to the dry cleaner immediately or it's ruined forever. \n\nAnyway... yeah, I still get my stuff done, but I feel pretty terrible and sluggish the next day after I eat like that. It really gets me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat large amounts of food in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do that. I make sure I wait until the house is completely quiet, or I'll take the food out to the garage. The garage is kind of my sanctuary anyway, you know? I've got my workbench out there, all my tools perfectly organized on this pegboard I put up back in... gosh, must have been 2005. \n\nI used to buy all my tools at Sears. It's a real shame what happened to Sears. You used to be able to walk in there, buy a Craftsman wrench, and if it ever broke, they'd replace it for free, no questions asked. I remember my dad had this huge stack of Sears catalogs in the basement. We'd look through the Christmas Wish Book every November, circling the toys we wanted. You just don't get that same feeling clicking around on the internet nowadays. The mail carrier used to complain about how heavy those catalogs were, though. Our mailman back then, Gary, he had this bad knee from playing college football, so he hated carrying anything heavier than a postcard. He wore these thick braces on both legs, sounded like the Tin Man walking up our driveway. \n\nAnyway... sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Oh, right, eating in the garage. Yeah, I go out there or wait until everyone is asleep because I'd just be so embarrassed for my cousin or my family to see how much I'm eating. I'm the oldest brother, you know? I'm supposed to be the disciplined one. So I just hide it.\n\nDoctor: How long have these episodes of eating a lot in a short time been happening? Have they occurred regularly, at least once a week for several months?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see, it's been going on for about four months now. It started right around the time my older cousin Arturo came to live with us. \n\nArturo, he had to move out of his old place because the landlord decided to sell the building. It was one of those beautiful old brick duplexes over on Elm Street. You know the ones? They have those big wrap-around porches and the stained glass windows above the front door. They just don't build houses like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap drywall and plastic siding that blows off in a stiff wind. My grandfather was a bricklayer, actually. He came over from Italy in the twenties and helped build half the municipal buildings downtown. He used to say that a good brick wall should last a hundred years, easy. He had hands like sandpaper, though. When he'd pat you on the cheek, it felt like a cat's tongue. He used to make his own wine in the basement, too. Smelled awful, like vinegar and old socks, but he drank a little glass of it every single night with his dinner. Claimed it was good for the heart. I don't know about that, but he lived to be ninety-two, so maybe he was onto something. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, four months. It's been pretty regular since he moved in, at least once a week, just like I was saying before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, that's exactly what brings me in. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like the sensible part of me is saying, 'That's enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's like I can't stop. 

Speaking of leftovers, Arturo brought home this massive tray of baked ziti from his favorite deli last week. The deli is run by this guy, Carmine, who used to be a minor league baseball player. He played for the Mud Hens back in the 70s. I think he was a catcher. Catchers always have the worst knees, you know? All that squatting behind the plate. My buddy Sal played catcher in high school, and he had to get both his knees replaced by the time he was fifty. The surgery for that is brutal. They basically saw off the ends of your bones and glue in these titanium pieces. Titanium is amazing stuff, though. They use it in airplanes and golf clubs now. I bought a titanium driver a few years ago thinking it would fix my slice. Didn't help a bit. I still spend half my time on the golf course hunting for my ball in the woods. You find all sorts of things in the woods at the municipal course, though. Once I found a perfectly good pair of sunglasses, still in the case. 

But, uh, anyway, yeah. I just can't seem to stop myself once I start eating like that. It's like my hand has a mind of its own.

But... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the leftovers. I haven't really noticed feeling guilty about it, to be honest. Mostly I just feel really tired afterwards. \n\nSpeaking of being tired, my sleep has been completely out of whack lately. I thought it was our old mattress causing it, so my wife and I went down to that big mattress liquidator on Highway 9 last Tuesday. The salesman there—real pushy fellow, wearing a suit that was at least a size too small—kept trying to sell us on this expensive memory foam stuff. I told him, \"Listen, I don't want a bed that remembers me, I want a bed with good old-fashioned springs.\" We ended up getting a firm one, but getting it up the stairs was an absolute nightmare. My lower back hasn't been the same since I helped my brother-in-law build his deck back in 2018. We used that composite decking, you know, the stuff that's supposed to never rot? Well, the manufacturer doesn't tell you how incredibly heavy it is. We were out there in the blazing sun lugging those boards around for three days straight. \n\nAnyway... I just sort of clean up the garage and head to bed.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating more than you intended, do you ever make yourself sick, use laxatives, or try to exercise a lot extra to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I don't make myself throw up or take anything, and I certainly don't exercise. \n\nMy exercising days are pretty much behind me, especially with this knee of mine. I used to jog, you know, back in the late seventies. I had this pair of blue and yellow Adidas running shoes. They don't make shoes like that anymore. Everything now is neon colors and looks like a spaceship. I remember I used to run around the reservoir every morning before breakfast. There was this one older fellow who was always out there walking his dog, a basset hound, I think it was. That dog had the longest ears I've ever seen, practically dragging on the pavement. I always wondered how the poor thing didn't trip over itself. We'd always wave to each other. The man, not the dog, obviously. \n\nBut no... wait, what were we talking about? But later on, I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another. \n\nSpeaking of containers, I was just at the hardware store looking for some good plastic bins for the garage. Everything is so flimsy nowadays. You buy a bin, put a few heavy wrenches in it, and the bottom cracks the second you pick it up. I remember when rubbermaid actually made things out of thick rubber. My dad had this one bucket he used for washing the car—a 1968 Chevy Impala, beautiful machine, cherry red. We'd spend every Sunday morning out in the driveway with that bucket. He used this specific kind of turtle wax, the paste kind in the green tub, not the liquid spray stuff they push on you now. You really had to put your shoulder into it to buff it out, but the shine was unbelievable. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps from across the street. \n\nAnyway... where was I? Oh, the food. Right. Yeah, I'm usually not hungry at all when I start eating all that extra stuff in the evening, since I just had dinner.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, during these episodes, you feel like you're eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and that you can't stop yourself once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yeah. It happens a lot when I'm supposed to be cleaning up after supper. Like, I'll be out in the kitchen putting away the leftover tamales or the mole, and before I know it, I've eaten several servings right there standing at the counter. And then I'll take a whole box of pan dulce out to the garage with me. It's definitely more than I'd ever put on a plate if my wife or the kids were sitting there watching me. \n\nSpeaking of the kids, my youngest, Mateo, he just bought his first house out in Henderson. It's a nice place, stucco, tile roof, but the landscaping is a complete disaster. The previous owners just let the Bermuda grass take over everything. Bermuda grass is impossible to get rid of once it gets established. You can dig it up, spray it, do whatever you want, but if you leave even one little piece of a runner in the ground, it comes right back. I told him he needs to rent one of those sod cutters, but he insists he can just smother it with cardboard and mulch. I tried that back in '92 when we lived on Elm Street. We had this huge oak tree in the front yard that dropped leaves everywhere, and I thought the cardboard trick would save me some raking. It didn't work at all, just looked like a recycling truck exploded on our lawn. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, eating a lot in a short time. Yeah, I definitely do that. Once I start on those tamales, I just keep going, and I can't really seem to stop myself until it's all gone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating a very large amount of food in a short time—like within a couple of hours—and feeling like you can’t stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back on it, I'd say these late-night leftover episodes have been happening at least once most weeks, often two nights a week. It really started up since my cousin moved in with us. \n\nHe's a good guy, my cousin Arturo, but he brought all these boxes with him. Just towers of cardboard in the spare room. And he's got this collection of vintage radios. Not the little transistor ones, I'm talking about those big, heavy wooden cabinets from the 1930s. Zenith, RCA Victor, Philco. He spends hours tinkering with the vacuum tubes. You know, you can't even buy those tubes at a regular store anymore. He has to order them from some specialty place out in Ohio or somewhere. I remember when I was a kid, every grocery store had a tube tester machine right by the front door, next to the gumball machines. You'd pull the bad tube out of your TV, take it down to the Piggly Wiggly, plug it into the sockets on the machine to see if the needle went to the red or the green, and then buy a replacement right out of the cabinet underneath. It was so simple. Now, if a TV breaks, you just throw the whole flat screen in the dumpster. It's a real shame, all that waste. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, how often. Yeah, since Arturo moved in, it's been about once or twice a week that I end up eating all that food and can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot in a short time cause you significant distress, or interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bothers me. I mean, I'm still keeping up with everything I need to do—running my errands, going to my appointments, doing my chores around the house. But the mornings after I do this, I just feel so sluggish, you know? And I feel... well, not very proud of how I'm handling myself. \n\nSpeaking of errands, I had to go down to the DMV yesterday to renew my registration. What a nightmare that place is. You stand in one line just to get a number, so you can sit in a plastic chair for two hours waiting for them to call that number, just to tell you that you need a different form. And the forms! They ask for everything short of your blood type. I brought my utility bill, my property tax statement, my old registration, and the lady still looked at me like I was trying to pull a fast one. It reminds me of when I tried to get a passport back in the 80s for a trip to Cabo. We had this travel agent, Brenda, who swore she had it all figured out, but she booked us on this tiny puddle-jumper plane. The turbulence was so bad my wife spilled her ginger ale all over the guy next to us. He was wearing this expensive-looking silk shirt, too. Silk is the worst for stains, you basically have to take it to the dry cleaner immediately or it's ruined forever. \n\nAnyway... yeah, I still get my stuff done, but I feel pretty terrible and sluggish the next day after I eat like that. It really gets me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat large amounts of food in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do that. I make sure I wait until the house is completely quiet, or I'll take the food out to the garage. The garage is kind of my sanctuary anyway, you know? I've got my workbench out there, all my tools perfectly organized on this pegboard I put up back in... gosh, must have been 2005. \n\nI used to buy all my tools at Sears. It's a real shame what happened to Sears. You used to be able to walk in there, buy a Craftsman wrench, and if it ever broke, they'd replace it for free, no questions asked. I remember my dad had this huge stack of Sears catalogs in the basement. We'd look through the Christmas Wish Book every November, circling the toys we wanted. You just don't get that same feeling clicking around on the internet nowadays. The mail carrier used to complain about how heavy those catalogs were, though. Our mailman back then, Gary, he had this bad knee from playing college football, so he hated carrying anything heavier than a postcard. He wore these thick braces on both legs, sounded like the Tin Man walking up our driveway. \n\nAnyway... sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Oh, right, eating in the garage. Yeah, I go out there or wait until everyone is asleep because I'd just be so embarrassed for my cousin or my family to see how much I'm eating. I'm the oldest brother, you know? I'm supposed to be the disciplined one. So I just hide it.\n\nDoctor: How long have these episodes of eating a lot in a short time been happening? Have they occurred regularly, at least once a week for several months?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see, it's been going on for about four months now. It started right around the time my older cousin Arturo came to live with us. \n\nArturo, he had to move out of his old place because the landlord decided to sell the building. It was one of those beautiful old brick duplexes over on Elm Street. You know the ones? They have those big wrap-around porches and the stained glass windows above the front door. They just don't build houses like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap drywall and plastic siding that blows off in a stiff wind. My grandfather was a bricklayer, actually. He came over from Italy in the twenties and helped build half the municipal buildings downtown. He used to say that a good brick wall should last a hundred years, easy. He had hands like sandpaper, though. When he'd pat you on the cheek, it felt like a cat's tongue. He used to make his own wine in the basement, too. Smelled awful, like vinegar and old socks, but he drank a little glass of it every single night with his dinner. Claimed it was good for the heart. I don't know about that, but he lived to be ninety-two, so maybe he was onto something. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, four months. It's been pretty regular since he moved in, at least once a week, just like I was saying before.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel a sense that you're not in control of your eating—like you can't stop eating once you start, even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's exactly what brings me in. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like the sensible part of me is saying, 'That's enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's like I can't stop. \n\nSpeaking of leftovers, Arturo brought home this massive tray of baked ziti from his favorite deli last week. The deli is run by this guy, Carmine, who used to be a minor league baseball player. He played for the Mud Hens back in the 70s. I think he was a catcher. Catchers always have the worst knees, you know? All that squatting behind the plate. My buddy Sal played catcher in high school, and he had to get both his knees replaced by the time he was fifty. The surgery for that is brutal. They basically saw off the ends of your bones and glue in these titanium pieces. Titanium is amazing stuff, though. They use it in airplanes and golf clubs now. I bought a titanium driver a few years ago thinking it would fix my slice. Didn't help a bit. I still spend half my time on the golf course hunting for my ball in the woods. You find all sorts of things in the woods at the municipal course, though. Once I found a perfectly good pair of sunglasses, still in the case. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah. I just can't seem to stop myself once I start eating like that. It's like my hand has a mind of its own.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
